I'm just keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: When you're moving from one task to the next all day, do you feel like you're doing it because you're worried about something, or is it more just a habit from your work routine?\n\nPatient: No, I'm not... I wouldn't say I'm worried about anything. It's probably just... habit, I guess. Just used to being on the move.\n\nDoctor: How has your mood been since you stopped working? Would you say you've been feeling down, sad, or empty for most of the day?\n\nPatient: No... I wouldn't say that. I feel fine, mostly. Just... normal, I suppose. I don't really get down like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant busyness been bothering you or causing you any problems in your daily life, or does it feel like something you're comfortable with?\n\nPatient: Well... I guess it's been a little bothersome. My wife, she's... she's had to start keeping the calendar for us. Checking up behind me on things. And, uh... I've had to apologize a few times down at the community center. And the garden group, too. Just... letting people down, I suppose. It's not great.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually good, excited, or 'on top of the world' — maybe for several days or more — where you needed much less sleep but still felt energized?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I just... I sleep fine. Never really felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Before you stopped working, would you say you've always been someone who likes to stay busy and keep moving, or is this more of a new thing that started after you left your job?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I've always kept busy. But it didn't really become a problem until lately. Just... after I retired from doing handyman work. That was about a year and two months ago, I think. It just got worse after that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don't really get down like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant busyness been bothering you or causing you any problems in your daily life, or does it feel like something you're comfortable with?\n\nPatient: Well... I guess it's been a little bothersome. My wife, she's... she's had to start keeping the calendar for us. Checking up behind me on things. And, uh... I've had to apologize a few times down at the community center. And the garden group, too. Just... letting people down, I suppose. It's not great.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually good, excited, or 'on top of the world' — maybe for several days or more — where you needed much less sleep but still felt energized?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I just... I sleep fine. Never really felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Before you stopped working, would you say you've always been someone who likes to stay busy and keep moving, or is this more of a new thing that started after you left your job?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I've always kept busy. But it didn't really become a problem until lately. Just... after I retired from doing handyman work. That was about a year and two months ago, I think. It just got worse after that.\n\nDoctor: Before you retired, did your wife typically manage things like the calendar and keep track of appointments, or is that something that's more recent since you've been home?\n\nPatient: I don't really know... I guess she's always handled some of that. I was just out working most days, so I didn't pay it much mind. Maybe she just does it more now. Hard to say.\n\nDoctor: Before you retired, did people ever mention that you had trouble paying attention to details, or that you tended to act without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: No... not that I remember. I mostly just worked by myself, doing my jobs. Nobody really complained about how I did things, so... I don't think so.\n\nDoctor: When you were working, would you say you were someone who liked things done a certain way, or who had high standards for how work should be completed?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I just did the work. If something was fixed, it was fixed. I didn't really fuss over it too much, I suppose. Just... got the job done and moved on to the next one.\n\nDoctor: When you were younger — say, in school or as a teenager — did teachers or family members ever say you had trouble sitting still, paying attention, or organizing your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I suppose they did. Back in grade school, mostly. Teachers used to tell my parents I was... bright, but scattered, I think was the word. Always losing my pencils and things. And... well, I guess I had a habit of just blurting out answers without raising my hand. They were always having to remind me to finish my assignments before I could go out for recess. So... yeah, I guess that was a thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that back in school you were scattered and had trouble sitting still. After you finished school and started working as a handyman, would you say you were still that way — always moving between tasks and having trouble slowing down — or did that settle down once you were working?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I just... did the work. Being a handyman, you're always moving around anyway, so... I guess I didn't notice if I was having trouble slowing down. It was just the job.\n\nDoctor: Since you retired about a year ago, have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy things you used to like doing, or do you feel less interested in hobbies or activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I still putter around the house. Fix things up here and there. I guess I still enjoy that well enough. Nothing's really changed much, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you were actively working as a handyman, did your wife or people close to you ever mention that you seemed restless, or that you had a hard time relaxing or sitting down when you weren't working?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. My wife never really said much about it. When I was off the clock, I was just... home. I don't remember anyone making a fuss about me not relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Now that you've been retired for over a year, have you found yourself getting frustrated or feeling like something's missing when you try to just sit and relax for an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I just sit and watch TV sometimes. It's fine, I guess. I don't feel like anything's missing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don't really get down like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant busyness been bothering you or causing you any problems in your daily life, or does it feel like something you're comfortable with?\n\nPatient: Well... I guess it's been a little bothersome. My wife, she's... she's had to start keeping the calendar for us. Checking up behind me on things. And, uh... I've had to apologize a few times down at the community center. And the garden group, too. Just... letting people down, I suppose. It's not great.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually good, excited, or 'on top of the world' — maybe for several days or more — where you needed much less sleep but still felt energized?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I just... I sleep fine. Never really felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Before you stopped working, would you say you've always been someone who likes to stay busy and keep moving, or is this more of a new thing that started after you left your job?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I've always kept busy. But it didn't really become a problem until lately. Just... after I retired from doing handyman work. That was about a year and two months ago, I think. It just got worse after that.\n\nDoctor: Before you retired, did your wife typically manage things like the calendar and keep track of appointments, or is that something that's more recent since you've been home?\n\nPatient: I don't really know... I guess she's always handled some of that. I was just out working most days, so I didn't pay it much mind. Maybe she just does it more now. Hard to say.\n\nDoctor: Before you retired, did people ever mention that you had trouble paying attention to details, or that you tended to act without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: No... not that I remember. I mostly just worked by myself, doing my jobs. Nobody really complained about how I did things, so... I don't think so.\n\nDoctor: When you were working, would you say you were someone who liked things done a certain way, or who had high standards for how work should be completed?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I just did the work. If something was fixed, it was fixed. I didn't really fuss over it too much, I suppose. Just... got the job done and moved on to the next one.\n\nDoctor: When you were younger — say, in school or as a teenager — did teachers or family members ever say you had trouble sitting still, paying attention, or organizing your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I suppose they did. Back in grade school, mostly. Teachers used to tell my parents I was... bright, but scattered, I think was the word. Always losing my pencils and things. And... well, I guess I had a habit of just blurting out answers without raising my hand. They were always having to remind me to finish my assignments before I could go out for recess. So... yeah, I guess that was a thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that back in school you were scattered and had trouble sitting still. After you finished school and started working as a handyman, would you say you were still that way — always moving between tasks and having trouble slowing down — or did that settle down once you were working?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I just... did the work. Being a handyman, you're always moving around anyway, so... I guess I didn't notice if I was having trouble slowing down. It was just the job.\n\nDoctor: Since you retired about a year ago, have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy things you used to like doing, or do you feel less interested in hobbies or activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I still putter around the house. Fix things up here and there. I guess I still enjoy that well enough. Nothing's really changed much, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you were actively working as a handyman, did your wife or people close to you ever mention that you seemed restless, or that you had a hard time relaxing or sitting down when you weren't working?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. My wife never really said much about it. When I was off the clock, I was just... home. I don't remember anyone making a fuss about me not relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Now that you've been retired for over a year, have you found yourself getting frustrated or feeling like something's missing when you try to just sit and relax for an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I just sit and watch TV sometimes. It's fine, I guess. I don't feel like anything's missing.\n\nDoctor: When you were working as a handyman, did you find yourself jumping from one job to another pretty quickly, or were you able to stick with one task for a good stretch of time before moving on?\n\nPatient: I usually just stuck with whatever job I was doing. If I was fixing a sink, I'd fix the sink. I didn't really jump around much. Just did the work until it was done.\n\nDoctor: When you were younger — say in your twenties or thirties when you first started doing handyman work — did people ever tell you that you were restless, or that you seemed to need to keep moving and stay busy even when you weren't on a job?\n\nPatient: No... not that I recall. That was a long time ago, but I don't remember anyone saying anything like that. I just did my work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's not great.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually good, excited, or 'on top of the world' — maybe for several days or more — where you needed much less sleep but still felt energized?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I just... I sleep fine. Never really felt like that.\n\nDoctor: Before you stopped working, would you say you've always been someone who likes to stay busy and keep moving, or is this more of a new thing that started after you left your job?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I've always kept busy. But it didn't really become a problem until lately. Just... after I retired from doing handyman work. That was about a year and two months ago, I think. It just got worse after that.\n\nDoctor: Before you retired, did your wife typically manage things like the calendar and keep track of appointments, or is that something that's more recent since you've been home?\n\nPatient: I don't really know... I guess she's always handled some of that. I was just out working most days, so I didn't pay it much mind. Maybe she just does it more now. Hard to say.\n\nDoctor: Before you retired, did people ever mention that you had trouble paying attention to details, or that you tended to act without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: No... not that I remember. I mostly just worked by myself, doing my jobs. Nobody really complained about how I did things, so... I don't think so.\n\nDoctor: When you were working, would you say you were someone who liked things done a certain way, or who had high standards for how work should be completed?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I just did the work. If something was fixed, it was fixed. I didn't really fuss over it too much, I suppose. Just... got the job done and moved on to the next one.\n\nDoctor: When you were younger — say, in school or as a teenager — did teachers or family members ever say you had trouble sitting still, paying attention, or organizing your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I suppose they did. Back in grade school, mostly. Teachers used to tell my parents I was... bright, but scattered, I think was the word. Always losing my pencils and things. And... well, I guess I had a habit of just blurting out answers without raising my hand. They were always having to remind me to finish my assignments before I could go out for recess. So... yeah, I guess that was a thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that back in school you were scattered and had trouble sitting still. After you finished school and started working as a handyman, would you say you were still that way — always moving between tasks and having trouble slowing down — or did that settle down once you were working?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I just... did the work. Being a handyman, you're always moving around anyway, so... I guess I didn't notice if I was having trouble slowing down. It was just the job.\n\nDoctor: Since you retired about a year ago, have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy things you used to like doing, or do you feel less interested in hobbies or activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I still putter around the house. Fix things up here and there. I guess I still enjoy that well enough. Nothing's really changed much, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: When you were actively working as a handyman, did your wife or people close to you ever mention that you seemed restless, or that you had a hard time relaxing or sitting down when you weren't working?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. My wife never really said much about it. When I was off the clock, I was just... home. I don't remember anyone making a fuss about me not relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Now that you've been retired for over a year, have you found yourself getting frustrated or feeling like something's missing when you try to just sit and relax for an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I just sit and watch TV sometimes. It's fine, I guess. I don't feel like anything's missing.\n\nDoctor: When you were working as a handyman, did you find yourself jumping from one job to another pretty quickly, or were you able to stick with one task for a good stretch of time before moving on?\n\nPatient: I usually just stuck with whatever job I was doing. If I was fixing a sink, I'd fix the sink. I didn't really jump around much. Just did the work until it was done.\n\nDoctor: When you were younger — say in your twenties or thirties when you first started doing handyman work — did people ever tell you that you were restless, or that you seemed to need to keep moving and stay busy even when you weren't on a job?\n\nPatient: No... not that I recall. That was a long time ago, but I don't remember anyone saying anything like that. I just did my work.\n\nDoctor: Since you retired, when you're trying to do one task — like fix something specific in the house — do you find yourself stopping partway through and starting something else, or do you usually finish what you started?\n\nPatient: I guess I stop sometimes. Like... my wife will ask me to fix the gate, sweep the porch, put the tools away. And I'll get the gate maybe half done, and then... I don't know, I'll wander off to pull weeds. I just leave the rest sitting there."
